## 5.3Controlled simulations

We simulateT=12T=12snapshots onn=300n=300nodes in three regimes of four snapshots each: a
community regime (a three-block SBM), a hub regime (a Chung–Lu power-law degree model),
and a geometry regime (a two-dimensional GRDPG). This is a controlled setting in which the
data-generating mechanism changes abruptly, and it is the natural test of whether the
synthesis can track. Table5reports averages over the eleven one-step
forecasts.Table 5:Regime-switching simulation (n=300n=300,T=12T=12, three regimes). Averages over the
eleven one-step-ahead forecasts.MethodNLLPIT-KSECEDynamic BPS0.7420.0740.108BMA1.4430.4010.433Stacking1.3830.3910.426Equal weight1.3780.3890.426SBM agent1.4270.4030.432GRDPG agent2.3380.3650.417Chung–Lu agent1.4300.4000.431

The synthesis roughly halves the NLL of every competitor (NLL0.740.74versus1.381.38–2.342.34) and improves calibration by a factor of five (PIT-KS0.0740.074versus0.390.39–0.400.40). The
per-regime synthesis log-scores are stable (0.830.83community,0.700.70hub,0.710.71geometry),
showing that no single regime is responsible for the gain.

The filtered log-score and weights reflect the regime structure. The one-step log-score spikes at each
regime change (snapshotst=4t=4andt=8t=8) and re-adapts within the regime, whereas BMA and
stacking remain high throughout, and the filtered weights reallocate in step: in the community
regime the SBM weight is large (≈0.9\approx 0.9); at the switch to the hub regime the Chung–Lu
weight rises from0.120.12to0.750.75while SBM is discounted; and the weights move again at the
geometry switch.

Theorem3sharpens this into a localization claim: when a mechanism is active with
a positive margin, the largest filtered weight should name it. Scoring the argmax of the
filtered structural weights against the active regime across the eleven forecasts, the
synthesis localizes correctly in66of1111snapshots, and the pattern is exactly what the
margin condition predicts. In the community regime the SBM weight is largest at all three
forecasts (3/33/3); in the hub regime Chung–Lu is largest at three of four, the single miss
being the first snapshot after the switch, which is the recovery delayhhof the theorem
made visible. In the geometry regime the GRDPG weight is not the largest in any of the four geometry-regime forecasts. The
latent-geometry signal in this simulation is weakly separated from degree heterogeneity, so the
marginκ\kappais small and the localization condition of Theorem3fails. This is consistent with the theorem, which guarantees localization only above the margin; and the design-conditioning
diagnostic of Section5.3is what tells a practitioner in advance which regimes
are separable. Calibration, by contrast, does not depend on the margin and remains strong
throughout (PIT-KS0.0740.074).

Theorem3guarantees localization only above the margin, so the geometry failure above
should be a small-margin artifact, not a structural limit. We confirm this with the same
three-regime design at a large, well-separated per-dyad contrast (κ=2.2\kappa=2.2), with the three
signals made mutually distinct: assortative blocks for community, latent positions on the unit
sphere for geometry (unit norm, so the geometry signal carries no degree information and the earlier
aliasing is removed), and an expected-degree product for degree. Over thirty replications, at the
settled snapshot of each regime the largest synthesis weight lands on the truly active mechanism in
every case, a localization hit rate of1.001.00for all three mechanisms, geometry included
(Figure6); the only misses fall in the one or two snapshots immediately after a
switch, the recovery delayhhof the theorem, which lowers the all-snapshot rate to0.580.58and0.600.60for the two regimes that follow a switch. Localization therefore succeeds for every mechanism
once its margin clears the threshold, exactly as Theorem3predicts, and the weak-margin
geometry failure is the predicted small-κ\kapparegime, not a limitation of the synthesis.Figure 6:Well-separated three-regime localization (κ=2.2\kappa=2.2,n=180n=180, thirty replications). At
the settled snapshot of each regime the largest synthesis weight lands on the active mechanism in
every replication (hit rate1.001.00for community, geometry, and degree, solid bars); the all-snapshot
rate (hatched) is lower only because of the one-to-two-snapshot recovery delay after each switch. The
weak-margin geometry failure in the preceding three-regime simulation is thus the small-margin regime of
Theorem3, not a structural limitation of the synthesis.

Remark2makes the gap over averaging rules quantitative: after a regime of lengthLL, vanilla Bayesian
model averaging has accumulatedΘ​(L)\Theta(L)log-odds of evidence for the old mechanism and
needs anΩ​(L)\Omega(L)-long window to reverse, whereas the discounted synthesis forgets inO​(1/(1−δ))O(1/(1-\delta))snapshots independent ofLL. Dynamic model averaging(Rafteryet al.,2010)introduces a forgetting factor to reduce this
inertia, so we include it with forgetting matched to the synthesis discount, alongside vanilla
averaging. A network switches once from a community regime (an SBM) to a hub regime (a
Chung–Lu degree model) afterLLsnapshots, and we record, for each method, the recovery
delay, the number of post-switch snapshots until its largest combination weight points to the
new mechanism. VaryingLLfrom22to1616and averaging over eight replications,
Figure7(center) reports the recovery delay. The synthesis recovers in a constant single
snapshot at everyLL(delay slope0.000.00). Vanilla averaging takes longer the longer the old
regime lasted, its delay rising from near zero atL=2L=2to nineteen snapshots atL=16L=16(slope1.391.39), one extra snapshot of lag per snapshot of history. Dynamic model averaging falls
between the two: its forgetting factor bounds the inertia, so the delay plateaus near six
snapshots, not growing without limit (slope0.430.43), but it still does not match the
synthesis, because it remains an averaging rule that must accumulateO​(1/(1−δ))O(1/(1-\delta))snapshots
of new-regime evidence to overcome the evidence it has retained, whereas the synthesis carries
an unconstrained logit state with an intercept and relocates at once.

Theorem3bounds the filter’s cumulative one-step-ahead regret against the per-snapshot
oracle by (9), whose only series-growing term is the comparator movement, itself
controlled by the number of switches. At a fixed horizon the cumulative regret rises monotonically
with the number of switches, from0.680.68at none to1.621.62at six (slope0.160.16per switch, tracking
a movement budget that climbs from0.50.5to1313), while holding switches at zero and quadrupling the
horizon leaves it essentially flat (0.690.69atT=10T=10,0.740.74atT=44T=44): the filter charges per
switch, not per time point, exactly as (9) predicts.

## The localization threshold.

By Theorem3, the filter localizes the active mechanism only above a contrast threshold:
the localization delay is of orderlog⁡J/(Nt​κ2)\log J/(N_{t}\kappa^{2}), so a settled regime is identifiable onceκ\kappaexceedslog⁡J/Nt\sqrt{\log J/N_{t}}. We confirm this on a synthetic sequence that alternates a
community and a degree regime at a controlled contrastκ\kappa, with the geometry agent present as a
distractor. The settled localization hit rate is near the chance level1/J1/Jfor smallκ\kappaand
reaches one forκ≥0.75\kappa\geq 0.75. The crossing lies a constant factor above the information floorlog⁡J/(Nt​ρ​(1−ρ))≈0.11\sqrt{\log J/(N_{t}\rho(1-\rho))}\approx 0.11, the one-snapshot agent-estimation terman,ta_{n,t}of
(9). The per-snapshot average saturates lower, near0.630.63, because each switch costs a
snapshot or two of relocalization.

## The dyadic rate.

Theorem1predicts that the synthesis weights are estimable from a single snapshot with
standard error(n2​ρn)−1/2(n^{2}\rho_{n})^{-1/2}. Subsampling induced subgraphs of the densest S&P snapshot
(November 2015,55,21455{,}214edges) and refitting the one-snapshot synthesis on each, the empirical
standard error of the fitted weights falls along the dyadic rate (Figure7, left):
a fitted log-log slope of−0.535-0.535against the theoretical−1/2-1/2, the weight standard deviation
shrinking from0.180.18to0.0180.018as the edge information grows sixty-six-fold. The same decay appears
on the full snapshots, where the latent-geometry weight is pinned to standard error0.030.03on the
stressed November 2015 graph but carries errors five to twenty times larger on the sparse November
2017 graph (1,9861{,}986edges). The rate is a property of one graph, not of the series.Figure 7:The single-snapshot theory confirmed on the same data. (Left) Theorem1: the
weight standard error falls along the dyadic rate, fitted slope−0.535-0.535against the predicted−1/2-1/2(S&P 2015 stress snapshot, log-log against subsampled dyadic informationN≍n2​ρnN\asymp n^{2}\rho_{n}). (Center) Theorem3: the recovery delay after a switch is flat in
the prior-regime lengthLLfor the synthesis (slope0.000.00) while model averaging grows linearly
(slope1.391.39). (Right) Theorem2: the detectable dyadic informationn2​ρn∗n^{2}\rho_{n}^{\ast}scales asδ−2\delta^{-2}in the separationδ\delta(fitted−1.93-1.93), the boundary below which two
mechanisms cannot be distinguished.

## Coverage and normality of the weights.

The rate is one half of Theorem1; the other half is the conditional normal limit
(8) with the sandwich variance, which is what licenses confidence intervals for
the weights. We test it in a controlled design where the population weight is known. Two
fixed agent surfaces, a three-block community logit and a degree logit, are combined at a
known weight𝜷∘=(α,θcomm,θdeg)\bm{\beta}^{\circ}=(\alpha,\theta_{\mathrm{comm}},\theta_{\mathrm{deg}})with alog⁡ρn\log\rho_{n}offset, and we sample many graphs from the resulting edge law, refit the dyadic
logistic regression on each, and form the inverse-information Wald intervals. Over400400replications atn=300n=300(Nt≈3,900N_{t}\approx 3{,}900), the empirical coverage of the nominal95%95\%intervals is96.3%96.3\%for the intercept,95.8%95.8\%for the community weight, and95.8%95.8\%for
the degree weight, and the standardized estimates(β^−β∘)/se(\widehat{\beta}-\beta^{\circ})/\mathrm{se}are indistinguishable from a standard normal (Kolmogorov–Smirnov statistics0.0490.049,0.0220.022,0.0290.029). The standard error of the community weight falls alongNt−1/2N_{t}^{-1/2}with a fitted
log-log slope of−0.500-0.500, the dyadic rate exactly. The intercept here is centered atlog⁡ρn+α\log\rho_{n}+\alpha, a direct illustration of
the sparsity offset: without it the intercept silently absorbs the density. With the agent
surfaces known, the conditional limit of Theorem1holds with valid coverage at the
predicted rate.

This coverage is conditional on the agents, and Theorem1is explicit that the
unconditional limit requiresNt​an,t→0\sqrt{N_{t}}\,a_{n,t}\to 0, a condition the SBM agent rate violates
in the denser part of the sparsity regime. We measure the cost directly. Refitting the
community and degree agents on each sampled graph and forming the same naive inverse-information intervals, the coverage of the community weight
falls below nominal and the shortfall grows with the edge information: from82%82\%atNt≈2,700N_{t}\approx 2{,}700to71%71\%atNt≈43,000N_{t}\approx 43{,}000, while the standardized bias rises from0.50.5to1.11.1standard errors over the same range. The fixed-agent coverage stays at nominal
throughout, so the gap is entirely the agent-estimation error entering the weight limit at
first order. The naive single-snapshot intervals are thus valid when the agents are known or
estimated at a negligible rate and undercover when a flexible agent is refit on the same dense
graph; the cross-fold orthogonal estimator of Theorem1ccloses this gap, with an
unconditional limit valid under ano​(Nt−1/4)o(N_{t}^{-1/4})agent rate.

## The cross-fold orthogonal correction.

Theorem1casserts that a cross-fold Neyman-orthogonal score restores an
unconditional normal limit, and valid intervals, under a mild agent rate; we confirm this on a
design where the orthogonal correction is exactly constructed. A known three-block community
surface carries the target weight, while the degree mechanism is the refit nuisance, estimated
on each graph by a regularized Fourier sieve in a node-pair covariate, and the blocks carry
distinct degree levels, so the target and the nuisance are correlated and a mis-estimated
nuisance contaminates the target. The naive plug-in, which treats the fitted sieve as known and
inverts the second-stage information, undercovers the community weight: the empirical coverage
of the nominal95%95\%interval lies between82%82\%and94%94\%, with a standardized bias as large
as one standard error where the nuisance bias is strongest. The cross-fold orthogonal estimator,
which partials the target against the nuisance basis out of fold and forms the orthogonal
sandwich, holds nominal coverage of96%96\%to98%98\%with a standardized bias below0.150.15across
the whole range of edge information, fromNt≈2,700N_{t}\approx 2{,}700toNt≈43,200N_{t}\approx 43{,}200. The
orthogonal coverage neither drifts nor degrades as the graph grows, which is the unconditional
limit of Theorem1cmade visible: the debiasing yields valid confidence
statements for an individual mechanism weight at the price of one cross-fold construction.

## The separation rate.

Theorem2predicts a separation rateΔn≍(n2​ρn)−1/2\Delta_{n}\asymp(n^{2}\rho_{n})^{-1/2}in graphon
log-odds, the boundary between distinguishable and indistinguishable mechanisms. Sweeping the log-odds
contrastδ\deltaand locating the boundaryn2​ρn∗n^{2}\rho_{n}^{\ast}at which power crosses0.90.9, the
boundary falls from683683to3939asδ\deltagrows from0.100.10to0.430.43(Figure7,
right), a fitted log-log slope of−1.93-1.93against the predicted−2-2. Every real snapshot, the S&P
graphs (1,9861{,}986to55,21455{,}214edges) and the co-occurrence graphs (700700to1,0001{,}000), lies well
inside the detectable region, the boundary sitting at vanishing contrast within the sparsity regime. The matching impossibility below the rate is
Theorem2itself, by the sparse-logit lemma and the two-point method.

## Dominance over model averaging under misspecification.

Under misspecification a coherent Bayesian model-averaging posterior concentrates on the single
Kullback–Leibler-closest agent as edge information accumulates, its weight vector tending to(1,0,0)(1,0,0): this is Berk’s collapse, the correct behavior of a posterior but the wrong default for
combination, since it discards the other agents instead of reweighting them. The synthesis instead
retains and reweights all agents, and Theorem4makes the resulting log-score gap
(11) strictly positive whenever the truth is not a single agent. In this misspecified
setting the gap is positive on every study, ranging from0.390.39on the S&P network to3.333.33on
the power-grid mesh; FigureS1in the Supplement plots the gap and the entropy collapse.

## Mechanism aliasing.

By Theorem1, two mechanisms are indistinguishable from a snapshot exactly when the
dyad-feature information matrixHtH_{t}is singular. Its condition numberκ​(Ht)\kappa(H_{t})is an observable
diagnostic of how close two mechanisms are to aliasing. The controls behave as predicted: a degree-corrected block
model, whose community and degree agents are distinct, is far better conditioned
(κ≈340\kappa\approx 340) than the near-aliased control, whereas a structureless Erdős–Rényi graph, where both agents
collapse to a constant and alias, is roughly six times worse. The real networks span four
orders of magnitude, from the dense S&P stress snapshot (κ≈6\kappa\approx 6), where distinct
community, geometry, and degree signals make the weights sharply separable and underwrite the
precise estimates of Section5.3, to the extremely sparse ca-GrQc
(κ≈7.8×104\kappa\approx 7.8\times 10^{4}), which approaches aliasing, so that individual weights there are
only weakly identified even where the pooled forecast is accurate.

## 5.4Large dynamic networks

The networks so far have a few hundred to a few thousand active nodes. To probe scale and
out-of-sample behavior we apply the same synthesis model, with the data construction and evaluation adapted for scale (symmetrization, balanced dyad sampling, and recent-history pruning, described below), to two
large public timestamped networks(Rossi and Ahmed,2015)on their entire node sets: the arXiv
HEP-PH citation network(Leskovecet al.,2007)(n=28,093n=28{,}093,T=85T=85monthly snapshots), the
Enron e-mail network(Klimt and Yang,2004)(n=86,978n=86{,}978,T=46T=46). The HEP-PH citation and
Enron e-mail logs are natively directed; because the model and theory of
Section2are defined for undirected graphs, we symmetrize each to fit the undirected
observation model, placing an undirected edge between two nodes joined by at least one citation,
message, or interaction within a snapshot and discarding the directional signal. The full dyad set is too large to score at these node counts. Each forecast is therefore evaluated on
a balanced dyad sample of all edges and an equal number of randomly sampled non-edges. The sparse offset maps the sample probabilities to the population scale as in
Corollary5, and all methods are scored on this common balanced sample. The three
mechanism agents are fit on the discounted-average adjacency throught−1t-1, so they are the dynamic block,
latent-geometry, and degree predictors used as competitors in the S&P study
(Section5.1) and match the history-based agent definition of Section2.
At this scale the discounted-average adjacency is pruned to its recent-history window so that the
largest graph fits in memory.

Two findings carry over to this scale (Table6, Figure8). First, the
synthesis is the best-calibrated method on both networks, with PIT deviation0.0310.031to0.0610.061and calibration error0.0410.041to0.0770.077, against0.410.41to0.500.50for every classical combiner and
single agent; on the log-score it is lowest among the compared methods on both networks, by2.52.5to3.53.5nats per dyad. Second, the weights remain tightly estimated at this scale: they favor degree and community
on the citation network, with the geometry weight near zero, and are positive across all three mechanisms on the e-mail network.
The two networks fall in different regimes: on the citation network the synthesis is calibrated and predictive, whereas the e-mail
network sits in the near-base-rate regime of Corollary5, where the edge probabilities are nearly uniform and the synthesis delivers calibration without discrimination.Table 6:Two large public timestamped networks, run with the same one-step-ahead pipeline on their
entire node sets; the three agents are fit on the discounted-average adjacency (the dynamic
predictors). Averages over the one-step-ahead
forecasts. Boldface marks the best in each column. The synthesis is the best-calibrated method on both networks and attains the
lowest log-score among the classical combiners and single agents compared here.MethodNLLPIT-KSECEarXiv HEP-PH(n=28,093n=28{,}093,T=85T=85)Dynamic BPS0.4130.0310.041BMA3.0320.4570.486Stacking2.9370.4480.484Equal weight3.0810.4490.487SBM agent3.0300.4570.486GRDPG agent3.8550.4240.479Chung–Lu agent3.9450.4700.495Enron e-mail(n=86,978n=86{,}978,T=46T=46)Dynamic BPS0.5970.0610.077BMA4.5080.4510.488Stacking4.1250.4470.488Equal weight4.1630.4570.491SBM agent4.6720.4950.499GRDPG agent4.5100.4310.479Chung–Lu agent4.6150.4740.496Figure 8:Two large dynamic networks (arXiv HEP-PH, Enron), agents fit on the
discounted-average adjacency. Top: one-step-ahead log-score on a logarithmic scale; the synthesis stays near11while
every classical combiner sits at33to55. Bottom: filtered mechanism weights with95%95\%confidence bands, estimable with tight intervals at these node counts; the degree and community weights lead on
the citation network and all three mechanisms carry positive weight on the e-mail network.

We also evaluate the one-step-ahead point forecasts by mean squared forecast error (MSFE1:T, the Brier score for a binary edge on the balanced evaluation set of all edges and an equal-sized random sample of non-edges). The synthesis attains0.1120.112and0.1650.165on the citation and e-mail networks; the classical combiners lie near0.470.47to0.500.50on both, worse by185%185\%to345%345\%, so the calibration advantage is also a point-forecast advantage.

## 6Discussion and limitations

We developed dynamic Bayesian predictive synthesis for dynamic networks. Each generative mechanism,
such as community structure, latent geometry, or degree heterogeneity, is treated as an agent that
forecasts the next snapshot’s edges, and a synthesis layer combines these forecasts with weights that
form the state of a dynamic linear model. The weights are the object of inference: the method
jointly estimates which mechanism is most informative at each time and produces a calibrated forecast,
specializing to calibrated link prediction at a single snapshot. Because a network onnnnodes has of ordern2n^{2}node pairs, one snapshot estimates the weights precisely even when the graph
is sparse, and faster than the mechanisms change. Conditional on the fitted agents the intervals hold
at the dyadic rate; for finite-dimensional agents a cross-fold orthogonal construction removes the
refitting bias and makes them unconditional, while for high-dimensional latent-position and degree
agents they remain conditional. Two mechanisms separate only above a sharp contrast threshold; a
change in the active mechanism is tracked at a cost paid only at the change, at a minimax-optimal
rate; and when no agent is correct the weight converges to the projection of the active mechanism onto
the set of agents while the forecast stays calibrated, where model averaging collapses onto a single
mechanism.

The empirical studies support these results. On real dynamic networks the estimated weights track
regime changes identified independently of the model; the synthesis remains calibrated where model
averaging collapses and pooling miscalibrates; and its advantage is largest at the regime changes
the theory identifies. The contribution is a single model that produces calibrated forecasts
without a separate recalibration step, together with interpretable time-varying mechanism weights
that carry conditional intervals, unconditional for finite-dimensional mechanisms, and identify mechanism changes. A forecaster that is not
mechanism-based can be recalibrated to match the probabilities, but does not provide the weights or
their uncertainty.

The results rest on two modeling choices: a finite, fixed set of agents, and an undirected binary
observation model with conditionally independent dyads. Enlarging the set of agents leaves the
filter unchanged but slows the estimation rate to{(n2​ρn)/Jt}−1/2\{(n^{2}\rho_{n})/J_{t}\}^{-1/2}and raises the
separation threshold. The conditionally independent dyad assumption holds only approximately for the
main-text networks, so the weight inference is validated on matched simulations and reported with a
dependence-robust variance.

We propose three extensions. First, weighted or count-valued edges replace the Bernoulli model with
a Gaussian or Poisson one. Directed and signed networks require asymmetric observation models and are
left to future work: the citation and e-mail networks studied here are natively directed and are
symmetrized to fit the undirected model, so a native-directed analysis, which must also model the
reciprocity-induced dependence between the two orientations of a dyad, is a genuine extension and not
a relabeling. The projection and tracking arguments extend to any observation model with a smooth
log-likelihood, but the directed case is not immediate. Second, the
Laplace filter can be replaced by particle filtering when a network snapshot is too sparse for the
Gaussian approximation. Third, the dynamic block-model and latent-space forecasters used here as
benchmarks(Xu and Hero,2014; Matias and Miele,2017; Sewell and Chen,2015; Durante and Dunson,2014)can
be folded into the set of agents.

## Data Availability Statement

All networks analyzed in this article are publicly available benchmark datasets. The main text uses a
financial correlation network built from public daily S&P 500 prices (Section5.1); the
SocioPatterns high-school and hospital-ward face-to-face contact networks(Fournet and Barrat,2014; Vanhemset al.,2013); and two large public timestamped networks(Rossi and Ahmed,2015), the arXiv HEP-PH citation network(Leskovecet al.,2007)and the Enron
e-mail network(Klimt and Yang,2004). The Supplement additionally analyzes the Bitcoin-OTC trust network(Kumaret al.,2016; Leskovec and Sosič,2016)and standard static link-prediction benchmarks, including the
ca-GrQc and ca-CondMat collaboration networks(Leskovecet al.,2007), the political-blogs network(Adamic and Glance,2005), and the Western-states power-grid network(Watts and Strogatz,1998).
Code reproducing all simulations, tables, and figures, together with the scripts that construct each
snapshot set from its public source, is openly available athttps://osf.io/fer69/overview?view_only=6f79f15628084adcb253f98b127d4649.

## Supplementary Material

This supplement contains the complete statements and proofs of the results of
Section 4 of the main text and of the two further inferential and tracking results. SectionS1proves the single-snapshot identification,
aliasing, and dyadic central limit theorem (Theorem 1). SectionS2proves the
sparse Bernoulli-logit perturbation lemma and the separation rate (Theorem 2).
SectionS3proves the point-prequential tracking, switch-recovery, and
localization theorem (Theorem 3). SectionS4proves the projection, calibration,
model-averaging collapse, and stacking-gap theorem (Theorem 4). SectionS5proves
the static cross-fitted synthesis corollary (Corollary 5). SectionS6proves the cross-fold orthogonal unconditional limit (Theorem 1c), and SectionS7proves the local minimax lower bound for mechanism tracking (Theorem 3b). Numbering of equations and
constants is local to each section; the constantCCmay change from line to line.

## Notation.

Notation matches the main text. The population log-odds projection of the network onto the
set of mechanism agents, written𝜷t∘\bm{\beta}^{\circ}_{t}in the main text, is denoted𝜷¯t,n\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{t,n}here; its oracle, error-free-agent counterpart is𝜷t,nor\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{or}}_{t,n}. The synthesis
dimension isd=J+1d=J+1; coefficient vectors are written in non-bold type; and a subscriptnnort,nt,nmakes the dependence on the snapshot size explicit. The information scaleNt,nN_{t,n}, the normalized information matrixHt,nH_{t,n}, the dyad featurezt,e=(1,ut,e(1),…,ut,e(J))⊤z_{t,e}=(1,u^{(1)}_{t,e},\dots,u^{(J)}_{t,e})^{\!\top}, the sparse offsetlog⁡ρn\log\rho_{n}, and the four
mechanism agents (block, geometry, degree, local) are exactly the objects of Section 4.

## Proof.

All probability statements in the main part of the proof are conditional onℱt,n\mathcal{F}_{t,n}. Thus the dyad set, the fitted-agent features, the
sparse offset, and the true probability array are fixed. We suppressttfrom the notation when no confusion is possible.
Conditional onℱt,n\mathcal{F}_{t,n}, the variables{Ye:e∈ℰn}\{Y_{e}:e\in\mathcal{E}_{n}\}are independent Bernoulli with success probabilitiespep_{e}. Since
the dyad sample is conditionally non-informative, the ordinary Bernoulli
likelihood is the correct conditional likelihood.
We first prove identification. Becauseσ\sigmais strictly increasing,qe​(𝜷)=qe​(𝜷′)q_{e}(\bm{\beta})=q_{e}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})for everye∈ℰne\in\mathcal{E}_{n}if and only if(𝜷−𝜷′)⊤​ze=0(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{\prime})^{\top}z_{e}=0for everyee.
For any𝜷∈Θ\bm{\beta}\in\Thetaanda∈ℝda\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a⊤​Hn​(𝜷)​a=Nn​(𝜷)−1​∑e∈ℰnwe​(𝜷)​(a⊤​ze)2.a^{\top}H_{n}(\bm{\beta})a=N_{n}(\bm{\beta})^{-1}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{n}}w_{e}(\bm{\beta})(a^{\top}z_{e})^{2}.

Sincewe​(𝜷)>0w_{e}(\bm{\beta})>0, this equals zero if and only ifa⊤​ze=0a^{\top}z_{e}=0for everyee.
ThereforeNull​{Hn​(𝜷)}=𝒩n{\rm Null}\{H_{n}(\bm{\beta})\}=\mathcal{N}_{n}, and the map𝜷↦{qe​(𝜷)}\bm{\beta}\mapsto\{q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\}is injective onΘ\Thetaiff𝒩n={0}\mathcal{N}_{n}=\{0\},
i.e. iffHn​(𝜷)H_{n}(\bm{\beta})is full rank. Ifa∈𝒩na\in\mathcal{N}_{n}, thenqe​(𝜷+s​a)=qe​(𝜷)q_{e}(\bm{\beta}+sa)=q_{e}(\bm{\beta})for every feasiblessandee, so the likelihood and risk
are constant along feasible null-space directions, whence𝜷\bm{\beta}is identified only modulo𝒩n\mathcal{N}_{n}. A linear functionalb⊤​𝜷b^{\top}\bm{\beta}is constant on𝜷+𝒩n\bm{\beta}+\mathcal{N}_{n}iffb⟂𝒩nb\perp\mathcal{N}_{n}, i.e.b∈𝒩n⟂=Range​{Hn​(𝜷)}b\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\perp}={\rm Range}\{H_{n}(\bm{\beta})\}. An affine dependence among the agent dyad-logit surfaces is a
nonzeroccwithc⊤​ze=0c^{\top}z_{e}=0for everyee, i.e.c∈𝒩nc\in\mathcal{N}_{n}; the
corresponding separate weights are aliased. This proves (a).
We now prove the rate and the CLT. By (B3),qe​(𝜷)​{1−qe​(𝜷)}≍ρnq_{e}(\bm{\beta})\{1-q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\}\asymp\rho_{n}uniformly overe,𝜷e,\bm{\beta}, soNn=∑eq¯e​(1−q¯e)≍mn​ρnN_{n}=\sum_{e}\bar{q}_{e}(1-\bar{q}_{e})\asymp m_{n}\rho_{n}withq¯e=qe​(𝜷¯n)\bar{q}_{e}=q_{e}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}); thusNn→∞N_{n}\to\infty, and ifmn≍n2m_{n}\asymp n^{2}thenNn≍n2​ρnN_{n}\asymp n^{2}\rho_{n}.
LetUn​(𝜷)=∑e{Ye−qe​(𝜷)}​zeU_{n}(\bm{\beta})=\sum_{e}\{Y_{e}-q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\}z_{e}be the empirical score, so∇ℓn​(𝜷)=−Un​(𝜷)\nabla\ell_{n}(\bm{\beta})=-U_{n}(\bm{\beta}). The conditional population score isΨn​(𝜷)=∑e{pe−qe​(𝜷)}​ze\Psi_{n}(\bm{\beta})=\sum_{e}\{p_{e}-q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\}z_{e}, andΨn​(𝜷¯n)=0\Psi_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=0, soUn​(𝜷¯n)=∑e(Ye−pe)​zeU_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=\sum_{e}(Y_{e}-p_{e})z_{e}with conditional covariance∑epe​(1−pe)​ze​ze⊤=Nn​Vn\sum_{e}p_{e}(1-p_{e})z_{e}z_{e}^{\top}=N_{n}V_{n}. Becausepe≤Cp​ρnp_{e}\leq C_{p}\rho_{n},‖ze‖≤B\|z_{e}\|\leq B, andNn≍mn​ρnN_{n}\asymp m_{n}\rho_{n}, theVnV_{n}are uniformly bounded, so for fixeddd,‖Un​(𝜷¯n)‖=Oℙ​(Nn)\|U_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N_{n}}).
The score derivative isU˙n​(𝜷)=−∑eqe​(𝜷)​{1−qe​(𝜷)}​ze​ze⊤\dot{U}_{n}(\bm{\beta})=-\sum_{e}q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\{1-q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\}z_{e}z_{e}^{\top}, so
the information isIn​(𝜷)=∑eqe​(𝜷)​{1−qe​(𝜷)}​ze​ze⊤I_{n}(\bm{\beta})=\sum_{e}q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\{1-q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\}z_{e}z_{e}^{\top}. By (C2),Gn⪰g​IdG_{n}\succeq gI_{d}, and by (B3) there isc>0c>0withqe​(𝜷)​{1−qe​(𝜷)}≥c​ρnq_{e}(\bm{\beta})\{1-q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\}\geq c\rho_{n}, soIn​(𝜷)⪰c​ρn​mn​Gn⪰c​g​mn​ρn​Id⪰λ​Nn​IdI_{n}(\bm{\beta})\succeq c\rho_{n}m_{n}G_{n}\succeq cg\,m_{n}\rho_{n}I_{d}\succeq\lambda N_{n}I_{d}uniformly over𝜷∈Θ\bm{\beta}\in\Theta, andIn​(𝜷¯n)=Nn​HnI_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=N_{n}H_{n}.

with probability tending to one, soQnQ_{n}is strongly convex with curvature of orderNnN_{n}.
Writingh=𝜷−𝜷¯nh=\bm{\beta}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}, Taylor’s theorem gives, for some𝜷h\bm{\beta}_{h}on the segment,Qn​(𝜷¯n+h)−Qn​(𝜷¯n)≥−‖h‖​{‖Un​(𝜷¯n)‖+‖∇Pn​(𝜷¯n)‖}+λ4​Nn​‖h‖2.Q_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}+h)-Q_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\geq-\|h\|\{\|U_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\|+\|\nabla P_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\|\}+\tfrac{\lambda}{4}N_{n}\|h\|^{2}.

Taking‖h‖=M​d/Nn\|h\|=M\sqrt{d/N_{n}}andMMlarge makes the right side positive on the boundary
of the ball with probability tending to one; since𝜷¯n\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}is interior andQnQ_{n}is
strongly convex, the minimizer lies inside, so‖𝜷^n−𝜷¯n‖=Oℙ​(d/Nn)\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}\|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{d/N_{n}}), which is (b) becaused=J+1d=J+1.
As‖𝜷^n−𝜷¯n‖=oℙ​(1)\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}\|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)and𝜷¯n\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}is interior,𝜷^n\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}is interior with probability tending to one, soUn​(𝜷^n)=∇Pn​(𝜷^n)U_{n}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=\nabla P_{n}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}). ExpandingUnU_{n}about𝜷¯n\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n},Un​(𝜷^n)=Un​(𝜷¯n)−Nn​Hn​(𝜷^n−𝜷¯n)+Rn,U_{n}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=U_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-N_{n}H_{n}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})+R_{n},

where, since the logistic third derivative is bounded by a constant times∑eqe​(𝜷)​{1−qe​(𝜷)}​‖ze‖3=O​(Nn)\sum_{e}q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\{1-q_{e}(\bm{\beta})\}\|z_{e}\|^{3}=O(N_{n}),‖Rn‖=Oℙ​(Nn​‖𝜷^n−𝜷¯n‖2)=Oℙ​(1)=oℙ​(Nn)\|R_{n}\|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N_{n}\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}\|^{2})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)=o_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N_{n}}).
Also‖∇Pn​(𝜷^n)‖≤‖∇Pn​(𝜷¯n)‖+sup𝜷‖∇2Pn​(𝜷)‖op​‖𝜷^n−𝜷¯n‖=oℙ​(Nn)\|\nabla P_{n}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\|\leq\|\nabla P_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\|+\sup_{\bm{\beta}}\|\nabla^{2}P_{n}(\bm{\beta})\|_{\rm op}\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}\|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N_{n}}).
Combining,Nn​Hn​(𝜷^n−𝜷¯n)=Un​(𝜷¯n)+oℙ​(Nn),N_{n}H_{n}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=U_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N_{n}}),

soNn​(𝜷^n−𝜷¯n)=Hn−1​Nn−1/2​Un​(𝜷¯n)+oℙ​(1),\sqrt{N_{n}}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=H_{n}^{-1}N_{n}^{-1/2}U_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

which is the asserted linear representation sinceUn​(𝜷¯n)=∑e(Ye−pe)​zeU_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=\sum_{e}(Y_{e}-p_{e})z_{e}.
For the normal limit, fixa∈ℝda\in\mathbb{R}^{d}and setXe,n=Nn−1/2​a⊤​ze​(Ye−pe)X_{e,n}=N_{n}^{-1/2}a^{\top}z_{e}(Y_{e}-p_{e}). These are conditionally independent, mean zero,
with|Xe,n|≤‖a‖​B/Nn→0|X_{e,n}|\leq\|a\|B/\sqrt{N_{n}}\to 0, so Lindeberg’s condition holds, and their variance
sums toa⊤​Vn​aa^{\top}V_{n}a. IfVn→VtV_{n}\to V_{t}, Lindeberg–Feller and the Cramér–Wold device giveNn−1/2​∑e(Ye−pe)​ze⇒N​(0,Vt)N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{e}(Y_{e}-p_{e})z_{e}\Rightarrow N(0,V_{t}); sinceHn→HtH_{n}\to H_{t}nonsingular,
Slutsky yieldsNn​(𝜷^n−𝜷¯n)⇒N​(0,Ht−1​Vt​Ht−1)\sqrt{N_{n}}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\Rightarrow N(0,H_{t}^{-1}V_{t}H_{t}^{-1}).
Under correct specificationpe=q¯ep_{e}=\bar{q}_{e}, soVn=HnV_{n}=H_{n}and the covariance isHt−1H_{t}^{-1}.
When the arrays are random, the argument applies to every deterministic realization satisfying
the assumptions, giving the quenched statement.
For the oracle target,Ψnfit​(𝜷¯n)=0\Psi^{\rm fit}_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})=0andΨnor​(𝜷t,nor)=0\Psi^{\rm or}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{or}}_{t,n})=0, so‖Ψnor​(𝜷¯n)‖=Oℙ​(Nn​an,t)\|\Psi^{\rm or}_{n}(\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N_{n}a_{n,t}); the mean-value theorem and the
oracle nonsingularity bound give‖𝜷¯n−𝜷t,nor‖=Oℙ​(an,t)\|\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{or}}_{t,n}\|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(a_{n,t}).
The triangle inequality with‖𝜷~n−𝜷^n‖=Oℙ​(rLap)\|\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{n}-\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}\|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(r_{\rm Lap})and
the rate in (b) gives the displayed three-term bound, and underNn​{an,t+rLap}→0\sqrt{N_{n}}\{a_{n,t}+r_{\rm Lap}\}\to 0the oracle-centered CLT has the same covariance.
∎

## S2Sparse Bernoulli-logit perturbations and the separation rate

The lemma below is the engine for the separation rate of Theorem 2. It quantifies, on the
sparse scale, how the Kullback–Leibler divergence, the Hellinger affinity, theχ2\chi^{2}divergence, and the log-likelihood-ratio variance between two Bernoulli laws scale with the
dyadic log-odds gap. The separation rate then follows by chi-square tensorization across
independent dyads together with the two-point method.

## Lemma S1(Sparse Bernoulli logit perturbations).

Letp,q∈(0,1)p,q\in(0,1), and writex=logit⁡(q)−logit⁡(p)x=\operatorname{logit}(q)-\operatorname{logit}(p).
Assume that, for fixed constants0<c0<C0<∞0<c_{0}<C_{0}<\inftyandη0<∞\eta_{0}<\infty,c0​ρ≤p,q≤C0​ρ≤12,|x|≤η0.c_{0}\rho\leq p,q\leq C_{0}\rho\leq\frac{1}{2},\qquad|x|\leq\eta_{0}.

Then there exist constants0<c<C<∞0<c<C<\infty, depending only onc0,C0,η0c_{0},C_{0},\eta_{0}, such thatc​p​(1−p)​x2≤D​{Bern⁡(q)‖Bern⁡(p)}≤C​p​(1−p)​x2,c\,p(1-p)x^{2}\leq D\{\operatorname{Bern}(q)\|\operatorname{Bern}(p)\}\leq C\,p(1-p)x^{2},c​p​(1−p)​x2≤−log⁡[p​q+(1−p)​(1−q)]≤C​p​(1−p)​x2,c\,p(1-p)x^{2}\leq-\log\!\left[\sqrt{pq}+\sqrt{(1-p)(1-q)}\right]\leq C\,p(1-p)x^{2},χ2​{Bern⁡(q),Bern⁡(p)}=(q−p)2p​(1−p)≤C​p​(1−p)​x2,\chi^{2}\{\operatorname{Bern}(q),\operatorname{Bern}(p)\}=\frac{(q-p)^{2}}{p(1-p)}\leq C\,p(1-p)x^{2},

and the same bounds hold withppandqqinterchanged. Moreover, ifZ=Y​log⁡qp+(1−Y)​log⁡1−q1−pZ=Y\log\tfrac{q}{p}+(1-Y)\log\tfrac{1-q}{1-p}withY∼Bern⁡(q)Y\sim\operatorname{Bern}(q), thenVar⁡(Z)≤C​p​(1−p)​x2.\operatorname{Var}(Z)\leq C\,p(1-p)x^{2}.

## Proof.

Sinceq=σ​{logit⁡(p)+x}=p​ex1−p+p​exq=\sigma\{\operatorname{logit}(p)+x\}=\frac{pe^{x}}{1-p+pe^{x}},q−p=p​(1−p)​(ex−1)1−p+p​ex.q-p=\frac{p(1-p)(e^{x}-1)}{1-p+pe^{x}}.

Under (L0),p≍q≍ρp\asymp q\asymp\rhoand1−p≍1−q≍11-p\asymp 1-q\asymp 1; and becausex∈[−η0,η0]x\in[-\eta_{0},\eta_{0}],
the mapx↦(ex−1)/xx\mapsto(e^{x}-1)/x(extended to11at0) is bounded above and below by positive
constants. Hence(q−p)2≍p2​(1−p)2​x2.(q-p)^{2}\asymp p^{2}(1-p)^{2}x^{2}.

For Bernoulli laws,D​{Bern⁡(q)‖Bern⁡(p)}=q​log⁡qp+(1−q)​log⁡1−q1−pD\{\operatorname{Bern}(q)\|\operatorname{Bern}(p)\}=q\log\tfrac{q}{p}+(1-q)\log\tfrac{1-q}{1-p}.
As a function ofqqit vanishes to first order atq=pq=pand has second derivativer−1​(1−r)−1r^{-1}(1-r)^{-1}at an intermediaterrbetweenppandqq; sincep≍q≍ρp\asymp q\asymp\rho,
Taylor’s theorem givesD​{Bern⁡(q)‖Bern⁡(p)}≍(q−p)2/{p​(1−p)}≍p​(1−p)​x2D\{\operatorname{Bern}(q)\|\operatorname{Bern}(p)\}\asymp(q-p)^{2}/\{p(1-p)\}\asymp p(1-p)x^{2},
which is (L1). With the Hellinger affinityA​(p,q)=p​q+(1−p)​(1−q)A(p,q)=\sqrt{pq}+\sqrt{(1-p)(1-q)},2​{1−A​(p,q)}=(p−q)2+(1−p−1−q)22\{1-A(p,q)\}=(\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q})^{2}+(\sqrt{1-p}-\sqrt{1-q})^{2}, so1−A​(p,q)≍(q−p)2/{p​(1−p)}≍p​(1−p)​x21-A(p,q)\asymp(q-p)^{2}/\{p(1-p)\}\asymp p(1-p)x^{2}; sincep,q≤12p,q\leq\tfrac{1}{2},AAis bounded away
from zero and−log⁡A≍1−A-\log A\asymp 1-A, which is (L2). The identityχ2​{Bern⁡(q),Bern⁡(p)}=(q−p)2/{p​(1−p)}\chi^{2}\{\operatorname{Bern}(q),\operatorname{Bern}(p)\}=(q-p)^{2}/\{p(1-p)\}with (L5) is (L3); the
interchanged bounds follow sincep≍qp\asymp q. Finallylog⁡(q/p)=x−log⁡{1+p​(ex−1)}=O​(x)\log(q/p)=x-\log\{1+p(e^{x}-1)\}=O(x)andlog⁡{(1−q)/(1−p)}=−log⁡{1+p​(ex−1)}=O​(p​x)\log\{(1-q)/(1-p)\}=-\log\{1+p(e^{x}-1)\}=O(px), so𝔼​Z2≤C​{q​x2+(1−q)​p2​x2}≤C​p​(1−p)​x2\mathbb{E}Z^{2}\leq C\{qx^{2}+(1-q)p^{2}x^{2}\}\leq C\,p(1-p)x^{2}, giving (L4).
∎

We now state the separation rate. Fix a snapshot and a dyad setℰn\mathcal{E}_{n}of sizemn≍n2m_{n}\asymp n^{2}. Letpep_{e}andqeq_{e}be two sparse edge-probability arrays on the scaleρn\rho_{n}, with per-dyad log-odds gapxe=logit⁡qe−logit⁡pex_{e}=\operatorname{logit}q_{e}-\operatorname{logit}p_{e},
and define the aggregate Fisher signalSn=∑e∈ℰnpe​(1−pe)​xe2.S_{n}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{n}}p_{e}(1-p_{e})x_{e}^{2}.

Under a uniform gap|xe|≍Δn|x_{e}|\asymp\Delta_{n}and the sparse scalepe​(1−pe)≍ρnp_{e}(1-p_{e})\asymp\rho_{n},Sn≍mn​ρn​Δn2≍n2​ρn​Δn2S_{n}\asymp m_{n}\rho_{n}\Delta_{n}^{2}\asymp n^{2}\rho_{n}\Delta_{n}^{2}.

## Theorem S2(Mechanism separation rate).

Assume the sparse scalec0​ρn≤pe,qe≤C0​ρn≤12c_{0}\rho_{n}\leq p_{e},q_{e}\leq C_{0}\rho_{n}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}and the bounded gapsupe|xe|≤η0\sup_{e}|x_{e}|\leq\eta_{0}of LemmaS1, with the dyads conditionally
independent. WritePn=⨂eBern⁡(pe)P_{n}=\bigotimes_{e}\operatorname{Bern}(p_{e})andQn=⨂eBern⁡(qe)Q_{n}=\bigotimes_{e}\operatorname{Bern}(q_{e}).
- (a)

(Achievability.)IfSn→∞S_{n}\to\infty, there is a testϕn\phi_{n}ofPnP_{n}versusQnQ_{n}with𝔼Pn​ϕn+𝔼Qn​(1−ϕn)→0\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\phi_{n}+\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}(1-\phi_{n})\to 0. In particular, the two
mechanism configurations are asymptotically distinguishable from a single snapshot whenevern2​ρn​Δn2→∞n^{2}\rho_{n}\Delta_{n}^{2}\to\infty.
- (b)

(Impossibility.)IfSn=O​(1)S_{n}=O(1), thenlim infninfϕ{𝔼Pn​ϕ+𝔼Qn​(1−ϕ)}>0\liminf_{n}\inf_{\phi}\{\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\phi+\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}(1-\phi)\}>0, where the infimum is over
all tests. The two configurations are mutually contiguous and cannot be separated with power
tending to one. In particular this holds whenevern2​ρn​Δn2=O​(1)n^{2}\rho_{n}\Delta_{n}^{2}=O(1).

Hence the critical separation rate isΔn≍(n2​ρn)−1/2\Delta_{n}\asymp(n^{2}\rho_{n})^{-1/2}: detection is possible
above it and impossible below it.

## Proof.

The argument is the two-point method, with the per-dyad divergence bounds supplied by
LemmaS1and aggregated across independent dyads.

Impossibility (b).By the tensorization identity for theχ2\chi^{2}divergence of product
measures,1+χ2​(Qn∥Pn)=∏e∈ℰn{1+χ2​(Bern⁡(qe)∥Bern⁡(pe))}≤exp⁡{∑e∈ℰnχe2},1+\chi^{2}(Q_{n}\,\|\,P_{n})=\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{n}}\left\{1+\chi^{2}\big(\operatorname{Bern}(q_{e})\,\|\,\operatorname{Bern}(p_{e})\big)\right\}\leq\exp\!\left\{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{n}}\chi^{2}_{e}\right\},

whereχe2=χ2​(Bern⁡(qe)∥Bern⁡(pe))\chi^{2}_{e}=\chi^{2}(\operatorname{Bern}(q_{e})\,\|\,\operatorname{Bern}(p_{e}))and we used1+u≤eu1+u\leq e^{u}. By LemmaS1(L3),χe2≤C​pe​(1−pe)​xe2\chi^{2}_{e}\leq C\,p_{e}(1-p_{e})x_{e}^{2}, so∑eχe2≤C​Sn=O​(1)\sum_{e}\chi^{2}_{e}\leq C\,S_{n}=O(1). Thereforeχ2​(Qn∥Pn)≤eC​O​(1)−1=O​(1)\chi^{2}(Q_{n}\,\|\,P_{n})\leq e^{C\,O(1)}-1=O(1). The
total variation distance obeysTV​(Pn,Qn)≤12​χ2​(Qn∥Pn)\mathrm{TV}(P_{n},Q_{n})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{\chi^{2}(Q_{n}\,\|\,P_{n})}, soTV​(Pn,Qn)\mathrm{TV}(P_{n},Q_{n})is bounded
away from one. Since for any test𝔼Pn​ϕ+𝔼Qn​(1−ϕ)≥1−TV​(Pn,Qn)\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\phi+\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}(1-\phi)\geq 1-\mathrm{TV}(P_{n},Q_{n}), with equality for the
likelihood-ratio test, the total testing error is bounded away from zero, proving (b).

Achievability (a).Consider the log-likelihood-ratio statisticΛn=∑e∈ℰn{Ae​log⁡qepe+(1−Ae)​log⁡1−qe1−pe},\Lambda_{n}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{n}}\left\{A_{e}\log\frac{q_{e}}{p_{e}}+(1-A_{e})\log\frac{1-q_{e}}{1-p_{e}}\right\},

and the testϕn=𝟏​{Λn>τn}\phi_{n}=\mathbf{1}\{\Lambda_{n}>\tau_{n}\}withτn=12​{𝔼Qn​Λn+𝔼Pn​Λn}\tau_{n}=\tfrac{1}{2}\{\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}\Lambda_{n}+\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\Lambda_{n}\}. WritingDnQ=D​(Qn∥Pn)=𝔼Qn​ΛnD_{n}^{Q}=D(Q_{n}\|P_{n})=\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}\Lambda_{n}andDnP=D​(Pn∥Qn)=−𝔼Pn​ΛnD_{n}^{P}=D(P_{n}\|Q_{n})=-\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\Lambda_{n},
LemmaS1(L1) and its interchanged form giveDnQ=∑eD​{Bern⁡(qe)‖Bern⁡(pe)}≥c​Sn,DnP=∑eD​{Bern⁡(pe)‖Bern⁡(qe)}≥c​Sn,D_{n}^{Q}=\sum_{e}D\{\operatorname{Bern}(q_{e})\|\operatorname{Bern}(p_{e})\}\geq c\,S_{n},\qquad D_{n}^{P}=\sum_{e}D\{\operatorname{Bern}(p_{e})\|\operatorname{Bern}(q_{e})\}\geq c\,S_{n},

so𝔼Qn​Λn−𝔼Pn​Λn=DnQ+DnP≥2​c​Sn→∞\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}\Lambda_{n}-\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\Lambda_{n}=D_{n}^{Q}+D_{n}^{P}\geq 2c\,S_{n}\to\infty. By
LemmaS1(L4) applied underQnQ_{n}, and its interchanged form underPnP_{n}, the per-dyad summands have variance at mostC​pe​(1−pe)​xe2C\,p_{e}(1-p_{e})x_{e}^{2}, so by independenceVarQn⁡(Λn)≤C​Sn,VarPn⁡(Λn)≤C​Sn.\operatorname{Var}_{Q_{n}}(\Lambda_{n})\leq C\,S_{n},\qquad\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}(\Lambda_{n})\leq C\,S_{n}.

UnderQnQ_{n},𝔼Qn​Λn=DnQ≥τn+c​Sn\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}\Lambda_{n}=D_{n}^{Q}\geq\tau_{n}+c\,S_{n}(sinceτn=12​(DnQ−DnP)≤12​DnQ≤DnQ−c​Sn\tau_{n}=\tfrac{1}{2}(D_{n}^{Q}-D_{n}^{P})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}D_{n}^{Q}\leq D_{n}^{Q}-c\,S_{n}forSnS_{n}large), and Chebyshev’s
inequality gives𝔼Qn​(1−ϕn)=ℙQn​(Λn≤τn)≤VarQn⁡(Λn)(DnQ−τn)2≤C​Sn(c​Sn)2=Cc2​Sn→0.\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}(1-\phi_{n})=\mathbb{P}_{Q_{n}}(\Lambda_{n}\leq\tau_{n})\leq\frac{\operatorname{Var}_{Q_{n}}(\Lambda_{n})}{(D_{n}^{Q}-\tau_{n})^{2}}\leq\frac{C\,S_{n}}{(c\,S_{n})^{2}}=\frac{C}{c^{2}S_{n}}\to 0.

Symmetrically, underPnP_{n},𝔼Pn​Λn=−DnP≤τn−c​Sn\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\Lambda_{n}=-D_{n}^{P}\leq\tau_{n}-c\,S_{n}, so𝔼Pn​ϕn=ℙPn​(Λn>τn)≤C/(c2​Sn)→0\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\phi_{n}=\mathbb{P}_{P_{n}}(\Lambda_{n}>\tau_{n})\leq C/(c^{2}S_{n})\to 0. Adding the two error
probabilities proves (a). The scaling statements follow fromSn≍n2​ρn​Δn2S_{n}\asymp n^{2}\rho_{n}\Delta_{n}^{2}under a uniform gap.
∎

## Remark S1.

The lemma supplies the sparse Bernoulli-logit perturbation bound used in the separation proof; the separation rate of TheoremS2is
obtained from it by the standard chi-square tensorization and two-point (Le Cam) reduction. The
optimal-test ceiling means the achievability test in (a) can be taken to be the likelihood-ratio
test, and the impossibility in (b) applies to every test, so the rate(n2​ρn)−1/2(n^{2}\rho_{n})^{-1/2}is
two-sided.

## S3Point-prequential tracking, switch recovery, and localization

## Loss, pseudo-truth, and information scale.

Letd=J+1d=J+1. At timett, letℰt\mathcal{E}_{t}be the held-out dyads used by the synthesis
update. Fore∈ℰte\in\mathcal{E}_{t}, defineηt,e​(𝜷)=ot,e+zt,e⊤​𝜷,qt,e​(𝜷)=σ​{ηt,e​(𝜷)},σ​(x)=ex1+ex,\eta_{t,e}(\bm{\beta})=o_{t,e}+z_{t,e}^{\top}\bm{\beta},\qquad q_{t,e}(\bm{\beta})=\sigma\{\eta_{t,e}(\bm{\beta})\},\qquad\sigma(x)=\frac{e^{x}}{1+e^{x}},

wherezt,e∈ℝdz_{t,e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}. Define the Bernoulli-logistic negative log-likelihoodℓt​(𝜷)=∑e∈ℰt[log⁡{1+exp⁡(ηt,e​(𝜷))}−At,e​ηt,e​(𝜷)],\ell_{t}(\bm{\beta})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{t}}\left[\log\{1+\exp(\eta_{t,e}(\bm{\beta}))\}-A_{t,e}\eta_{t,e}(\bm{\beta})\right],

and its conditional meanLt​(𝜷):=𝔼​{ℓt​(𝜷)∣ℱt−1}L_{t}(\bm{\beta}):=\mathbb{E}\{\ell_{t}(\bm{\beta})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\}.
The snapshot pseudo-true synthesis state is𝜷t∘∈arg​min𝜷∈ℬ⁡Lt​(𝜷)\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}}L_{t}(\bm{\beta}),
and the unnormalized excess expected log-loss isℛt​(𝜷):=Lt​(𝜷)−Lt​(𝜷t∘)\mathcal{R}_{t}(\bm{\beta}):=L_{t}(\bm{\beta})-L_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}).
In the per-dyad normalization of the main text and SectionS4, withRt​(𝜷)=mt−1​∑e∈ℰtKL​{Bern​(pt,e∗),Bern​(qt,e​(𝜷))}R_{t}(\bm{\beta})=m_{t}^{-1}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{t}}\mathrm{KL}\{\mathrm{Bern}(p^{*}_{t,e}),\mathrm{Bern}(q_{t,e}(\bm{\beta}))\}

andmt=|ℰt|m_{t}=|\mathcal{E}_{t}|, the excess risk isRt​(𝜷)−Rt​(𝜷t∘)=mt−1​ℛt​(𝜷)R_{t}(\bm{\beta})-R_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})=m_{t}^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{t}(\bm{\beta}); the tracking bounds below are stated in
the normalization-free excess log-lossℛt\mathcal{R}_{t}.
Define the snapshot information scaleNt:=∑e∈ℰtqt,e​(𝜷t∘)​{1−qt,e​(𝜷t∘)}.N_{t}:=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{t}}q_{t,e}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})\{1-q_{t,e}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})\}.

## Assumption 1(Regularity for point-state dynamic synthesis).

The following conditions hold uniformly fort≤Tt\leq T.
- (A1)

Predictability and cross-fitting.There is a filtration(ℱt)(\mathcal{F}_{t})such thatℰt\mathcal{E}_{t},ot,eo_{t,e},zt,ez_{t,e}, andpt,e∗p^{*}_{t,e}areℱt−1\mathcal{F}_{t-1}-measurable. Conditionally onℱt−1\mathcal{F}_{t-1}, the held-out dyads are
independent andAt,e∼Bernoulli​(pt,e∗)A_{t,e}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p^{*}_{t,e}),e∈ℰte\in\mathcal{E}_{t}.
- (A2)

Bounded sparse-safe design and first-order pseudo-truth.There is a constantK<∞K<\inftysuch thatsupt,e‖zt,e‖∞≤K\sup_{t,e}\left\lVert z_{t,e}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq K.
The parameter setℬ⊂ℝd\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}is compact and convex, all filtered states are projected
ontoℬ\mathcal{B}, and∇Lt​(𝜷t∘)=0\nabla L_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})=0(automatic if𝜷t∘\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}is interior).
- (A3)

Curvature and variance on the information scale.There exist0<m<M<∞0<m<M<\inftysuch that, for every𝜷∈ℬ\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{B},m​Nt​Id⪯∇2Lt​(𝜷)⪯M​Nt​IdmN_{t}I_{d}\preceq\nabla^{2}L_{t}(\bm{\beta})\preceq MN_{t}I_{d}, and the conditional score variance satisfies∑e∈ℰtpt,e∗​(1−pt,e∗)​zt,e​zt,e⊤⪯M​Nt​Id\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{t}}p^{*}_{t,e}(1-p^{*}_{t,e})z_{t,e}z_{t,e}^{\top}\preceq MN_{t}I_{d}.
- (A4)

Implementation perturbation.Let the ideal discounted logistic filter be𝜷~t∣t∈arg​min𝜷∈ℬ​∑s≤tδt−s​ℓs​(𝜷)\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}_{t\mid t}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}}\sum_{s\leq t}\delta^{t-s}\ell_{s}(\bm{\beta}),0<δ<10<\delta<1. The implemented cross-fitted Laplace filter satisfies, on an event𝒜T\mathcal{A}_{T},‖𝜷^t∣t−𝜷~t∣t‖2≤εt\left\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{t\mid t}-\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}_{t\mid t}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{t},t=1,…,Tt=1,\ldots,T; for the actual algorithm this event is proved separately, e.g. withεt≤an,t+rLap,t\varepsilon_{t}\leq a_{n,t}+r_{{\rm Lap},t}.

DefineΦt​(𝜷):=∑s≤tδt−s​Ls​(𝜷),Φ^t​(𝜷):=∑s≤tδt−s​ℓs​(𝜷),\Phi_{t}(\bm{\beta}):=\sum_{s\leq t}\delta^{t-s}L_{s}(\bm{\beta}),\qquad\widehat{\Phi}_{t}(\bm{\beta}):=\sum_{s\leq t}\delta^{t-s}\ell_{s}(\bm{\beta}),

By A3,∇2Φt​(𝜷)=∑s≤tδt−s​∇2Ls​(𝜷)⪰m​Ntδ​Id\nabla^{2}\Phi_{t}(\bm{\beta})=\sum_{s\leq t}\delta^{t-s}\nabla^{2}L_{s}(\bm{\beta})\succeq mN_{t}^{\delta}I_{d}, soΦt\Phi_{t}ism​NtδmN_{t}^{\delta}-strongly convex and𝜷¯t\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{t}is unique.Bias.Since∇Ls​(𝜷s∘)=0\nabla L_{s}(\bm{\beta}_{s}^{\circ})=0,∇Φt​(𝜷t∘)=∑s≤tδt−s​{∇Ls​(𝜷t∘)−∇Ls​(𝜷s∘)},\nabla\Phi_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})=\sum_{s\leq t}\delta^{t-s}\{\nabla L_{s}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})-\nabla L_{s}(\bm{\beta}_{s}^{\circ})\},

and the mean-value theorem with the A3 upper bound gives‖∇Ls​(𝜷t∘)−∇Ls​(𝜷s∘)‖2≤M​Ns​‖𝜷t∘−𝜷s∘‖2\|\nabla L_{s}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})-\nabla L_{s}(\bm{\beta}_{s}^{\circ})\|_{2}\leq MN_{s}\|\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}-\bm{\beta}_{s}^{\circ}\|_{2},
so‖∇Φt​(𝜷t∘)‖2≤M​∑s≤tδt−s​Ns​‖𝜷s∘−𝜷t∘‖2\|\nabla\Phi_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})\|_{2}\leq M\sum_{s\leq t}\delta^{t-s}N_{s}\|\bm{\beta}_{s}^{\circ}-\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}\|_{2}.
By optimality of𝜷¯t\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{t}and strong convexity at𝜷t∘\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ},m​Ntδ2​‖𝜷¯t−𝜷t∘‖22≤‖∇Φt​(𝜷t∘)‖2​‖𝜷¯t−𝜷t∘‖2\tfrac{mN_{t}^{\delta}}{2}\|\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{t}-\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\nabla\Phi_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})\|_{2}\|\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{t}-\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}\|_{2},
hence‖𝜷¯t−𝜷t∘‖2≤C​Btδ.\left\lVert\bar{\bm{\beta}}_{t}-\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}\right\rVert_{2}\leq CB_{t}^{\delta}.

∎Figure S1:Theorem4: dominance over model averaging under misspecification. (Left)
Log-score gap of the synthesis over Bayesian model averaging and stacking across seven theory-check
and benchmark studies (the switching and co-occurrence simulations, the S&P 500 network, polblogs,
ca-GrQc, ca-CondMat, and the power-grid mesh), positive on each. (Right) The averaging posterior collapses as edge information accumulates.
Within a single snapshot, the entropy of the Bayesian model-averaging weights over the three
mechanism agents falls fromlog⁡3\log 3toward zero as the number of scored dyads grows to≍n2​ρn\asymp n^{2}\rho_{n}, on both the switching simulation and the S&P network, as the posterior
concentrates on the single Kullback–Leibler-closest agent. The synthesis keeps all three agents
active throughout; because its coefficients are predictive log-odds weights, not a posterior
distribution, the contrast is qualitative, and the reference line marks the maximum (uniform) entropylog⁡3\log 3.

## S6Cross-fold orthogonal unconditional limit (Theorem 1c)

This section gives the full statement and proof of Theorem 1c of the main text. It specializes
the single-snapshot setting of SectionS1to a single snapshottt, suppressed from the notation,
and constructs a cross-fitted, Neyman-orthogonal version of the synthesis estimator whose normal
limit holds unconditionally over the dyad draw, the random fold split, and the agent refits.
Throughout,σ\sigmais the logistic link and the sparse offsetℓ=log⁡ρn\ell=\log\rho_{n}is carried
explicitly.

Fix a snapshotttand suppressttfrom the notation. Letℰn\mathcal{E}_{n}be the dyad set used in the one-snapshot synthesis
update,mn=|ℰn|m_{n}=|\mathcal{E}_{n}|, and letI1,…,IKI_{1},\ldots,I_{K}

be a random balanced partition ofℰn\mathcal{E}_{n}, with fixedK<∞K<\infty. Let𝒢n=σ​(𝒟n,I1,…,IK)\mathcal{G}_{n}=\sigma(\mathcal{D}_{n},I_{1},\ldots,I_{K})

be the sigma-field generated by the network design and the dyad split.
The design𝒟n\mathcal{D}_{n}contains the node-level latent quantities,
the dyad labels, and any nonrandom oracle agent limits.
Conditional on𝒢n\mathcal{G}_{n}, assume thatAe∼Bernoulli​(pe),e∈ℰn,A_{e}\sim{\rm Bernoulli}(p_{e}),\qquad e\in\mathcal{E}_{n},

independently over dyads. For each foldkk, let𝒯n,k=σ​(𝒢n,{Af:f∉Ik})\mathcal{T}_{n,k}=\sigma\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{n},\{A_{f}:f\notin I_{k}\}\bigr)

be the training sigma-field for foldkk. The cross-fitted nuisance
estimate used on foldkkis denoted byξ^−k\widehat{\xi}^{-k},
assumed𝒯n,k\mathcal{T}_{n,k}-measurable, so that the
validation dyads inIkI_{k}are not used to construct their own nuisance
estimate.
The nuisance object isξ=(η,ℓ),η=(u⋅(1),…,u⋅(J)),ℓ=log⁡ρn.\xi=(\eta,\ell),\qquad\eta=\left(u^{(1)}_{\cdot},\ldots,u^{(J)}_{\cdot}\right),\qquad\ell=\log\rho_{n}.

If the sparse offsetℓ\ellis fixed or conditioned upon, it may be
omitted fromξ\xi. Ifℓ\ellis estimated from the same snapshot,
then the fold-specific estimateℓ^−k\widehat{\ell}^{-k}must be included
inξ^−k\widehat{\xi}^{-k}, or its influence function must be appended to
the orthogonal score below.
For𝜷=(α,θ1,…,θJ)⊤∈ℝd,d=J+1,\bm{\beta}=(\alpha,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{J})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad d=J+1,

defineze​(ξ)=(1,ue(1),…,ue(J))⊤z_{e}(\xi)=(1,u^{(1)}_{e},\ldots,u^{(J)}_{e})^{\top}and the sparse-offset BPS probabilityqe​(𝜷,ξ)=σ​{ℓ+𝜷⊤​ze​(ξ)}.q_{e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)=\sigma\left\{\ell+\bm{\beta}^{\top}z_{e}(\xi)\right\}.

Letξ∘=(η∘,ℓ∘)\xi^{\circ}=(\eta^{\circ},\ell^{\circ})be the oracle nuisance, and let𝜷∘\bm{\beta}^{\circ}be the ordinary
sparse-offset BPS projection target. Writeqe∘=qe​(𝜷∘,ξ∘),we∘=qe∘​(1−qe∘),Nn=∑e∈ℰnwe∘.q^{\circ}_{e}=q_{e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ}),\qquad w^{\circ}_{e}=q^{\circ}_{e}(1-q^{\circ}_{e}),\qquad N_{n}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{n}}w^{\circ}_{e}.

For each fold,Nn,k=∑e∈Ikwe∘N_{n,k}=\sum_{e\in I_{k}}w^{\circ}_{e}withNn,k/Nn→πk∈(0,1)N_{n,k}/N_{n}\to\pi_{k}\in(0,1).
The ordinary BPS score isse​(𝜷,ξ)=ze​(ξ)​{Ae−qe​(𝜷,ξ)}s_{e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)=z_{e}(\xi)\{A_{e}-q_{e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)\},
the ordinary BPS population moment isΨns​(𝜷,ξ)=Nn−1​∑e∈ℰnE​{se​(𝜷,ξ)∣𝒢n},\Psi^{s}_{n}(\bm{\beta},\xi)=N_{n}^{-1}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{n}}E\{s_{e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\},

and the target𝜷∘\bm{\beta}^{\circ}satisfiesΨns​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)=0\Psi^{s}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})=0.
For each foldkk, letck,e​(𝜷,ξ)c_{k,e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)be a fold-specific correction, and define the foldwise orthogonal BPS
scoreψk,e⟂​(𝜷,ξ)=se​(𝜷,ξ)−ck,e​(𝜷,ξ),e∈Ik.\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)=s_{e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)-c_{k,e}(\bm{\beta},\xi),\qquad e\in I_{k}.

Define the foldwise and aggregate orthogonal population momentsΨn,k⟂​(𝜷,ξ)=Nn,k−1​∑e∈IkE​{ψk,e⟂​(𝜷,ξ)∣𝒢n},Ψn⟂​(𝜷,ξ)=Nn−1​∑k=1K∑e∈IkE​{ψk,e⟂​(𝜷,ξ)∣𝒢n}.\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta},\xi)=N_{n,k}^{-1}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\{\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\},\qquad\Psi^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta},\xi)=N_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\{\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\}.

The cross-fold orthogonal estimating equation isSn⟂​(𝜷)=∑k=1K∑e∈Ikψk,e⟂​(𝜷,ξ^−k).S^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta},\widehat{\xi}^{-k}).

## Theorem 1c(Cross-fold orthogonal unconditional BPS limit).

Assume the following conditions.
- (C1)

Sparse boundedness and information growth.There exist constants0<c<C<∞0<c<C<\inftysuch that, uniformly for𝜷\bm{\beta}in a neighborhood of𝜷∘\bm{\beta}^{\circ}andξ\xiin a
neighborhood ofξ∘\xi^{\circ},pe≤C​ρnp_{e}\leq C\rho_{n},c​ρn≤qe​(𝜷,ξ)≤C​ρn≤1/2c\rho_{n}\leq q_{e}(\bm{\beta},\xi)\leq C\rho_{n}\leq 1/2,
andsupe,ξ‖ze​(ξ)‖≤C\sup_{e,\xi}\|z_{e}(\xi)\|\leq C.
Moreover,mn≍n2,Nn=∑ewe∘≍mn​ρn≍n2​ρn,Nn→∞.m_{n}\asymp n^{2},\qquad N_{n}=\sum_{e}w^{\circ}_{e}\asymp m_{n}\rho_{n}\asymp n^{2}\rho_{n},\qquad N_{n}\to\infty.
- (C2)

Target preservation.For every𝜷\bm{\beta}in a neighborhood of𝜷∘\bm{\beta}^{\circ},Ψn⟂​(𝜷,ξ∘)=Ψns​(𝜷,ξ∘)\Psi^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta},\xi^{\circ})=\Psi^{s}_{n}(\bm{\beta},\xi^{\circ}),
equivalentlyNn−1​∑k∑e∈IkE​{ck,e​(𝜷,ξ∘)∣𝒢n}=0N_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\{c_{k,e}(\bm{\beta},\xi^{\circ})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\}=0.
Thus the orthogonal score has the same population target𝜷∘\bm{\beta}^{\circ}as the ordinary BPS score.
- (C3)

Foldwise Neyman orthogonality and second-order smoothness.For every foldkkand every admissible nuisance directionhh,∂ξΨn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)​[h]=0\partial_{\xi}\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})[h]=0.
Define the sparse Fisher nuisance norm‖h‖ℋn2=max1≤k≤K⁡Nn,k−1​∑e∈Ikwe∘​‖he‖2.\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}^{2}=\max_{1\leq k\leq K}N_{n,k}^{-1}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}w^{\circ}_{e}\|h_{e}\|^{2}.

There existsC<∞C<\inftysuch that, uniformly inkk,‖Ψn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ)−Ψn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)−∂ξΨn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)​[ξ−ξ∘]‖≤C​‖ξ−ξ∘‖ℋn2.\left\|\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi)-\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})-\partial_{\xi}\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})[\xi-\xi^{\circ}]\right\|\leq C\|\xi-\xi^{\circ}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}^{2}.
- (C4)

Construction of the correction.The correctionck,ec_{k,e}satisfies(C2)and(C3). A sufficient finite-dimensional construction is the
following. Supposeξ=ξ​(γ)\xi=\xi(\gamma), and letgk,e​(γ)g_{k,e}(\gamma)be a foldwise nuisance score satisfyingΓn,k​(γ∘)=Nn,k−1​∑e∈IkE​{gk,e​(γ∘)∣𝒢n}=0\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma^{\circ})=N_{n,k}^{-1}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\{g_{k,e}(\gamma^{\circ})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\}=0,
with nonsingular derivativeΓ˙γ,n,k=∂γΓn,k​(γ∘)\dot{\Gamma}_{\gamma,n,k}=\partial_{\gamma}\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma^{\circ}).
LetΨ˙γ,n,ks=∂γΨn,ks​(𝜷∘,γ∘)\dot{\Psi}^{s}_{\gamma,n,k}=\partial_{\gamma}\Psi^{s}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\gamma^{\circ}),
whereΨn,ks​(𝜷,γ)=Nn,k−1​∑e∈IkE​{se​(𝜷,γ)∣𝒢n}\Psi^{s}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta},\gamma)=N_{n,k}^{-1}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\{s_{e}(\bm{\beta},\gamma)\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\}.
Thenck,e​(𝜷,γ)=Ψ˙γ,n,ks​Γ˙γ,n,k−1​gk,e​(γ)c_{k,e}(\bm{\beta},\gamma)=\dot{\Psi}^{s}_{\gamma,n,k}\dot{\Gamma}_{\gamma,n,k}^{-1}g_{k,e}(\gamma)

is target-preserving atγ∘\gamma^{\circ}and satisfies the foldwise
orthogonality equation.
- (C5)

Cross-fitted nuisance rate.Forrn=maxk⁡‖ξ^−k−ξ∘‖ℋnr_{n}=\max_{k}\|\widehat{\xi}^{-k}-\xi^{\circ}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}},
we havern=op​(1)r_{n}=o_{p}(1)andNn​rn2=op​(1)\sqrt{N_{n}}\,r_{n}^{2}=o_{p}(1).
If the fold-specific nuisance population target differs fromξ∘\xi^{\circ}, the difference is included inrnr_{n}. Ifck,ec_{k,e}is estimated, the estimation error of the correction is also
included inrnr_{n}.
- (C6)

Cross-fit stochastic equicontinuity.LetΔk,e=ψk,e⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ^−k)−ψk,e⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)\Delta_{k,e}=\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\widehat{\xi}^{-k})-\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ}).
ThenNn−1/2​∑k=1K∑e∈Ik[Δk,e−E​{Δk,e∣𝒯n,k}]=op​(1).N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\left[\Delta_{k,e}-E\{\Delta_{k,e}\mid\mathcal{T}_{n,k}\}\right]=o_{p}(1).
- (C7)

Local root, local smoothness, and no aliasing.There exists a measurable local root𝜷~\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}such thatSn⟂​(𝜷~)=0S^{\perp}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}})=0and𝜷~−𝜷∘=op​(1)\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ}=o_{p}(1).
LetHn⟂=−∂𝜷Ψn⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)H^{\perp}_{n}=-\partial_{\bm{\beta}}\Psi^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ}).
There are constants0<λ<Λ<∞0<\lambda<\Lambda<\inftysuch thatλ≤λmin​(Hn⟂)≤λmax​(Hn⟂)≤Λ\lambda\leq\lambda_{\min}(H^{\perp}_{n})\leq\lambda_{\max}(H^{\perp}_{n})\leq\Lambda.
Moreover, withH¯n=−Nn−1​∫01∂𝜷Sn⟂​(𝜷∘+s​(𝜷~−𝜷∘))​d​s,\bar{H}_{n}=-N_{n}^{-1}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\bm{\beta}}S^{\perp}_{n}\left(\bm{\beta}^{\circ}+s(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ})\right)ds,

we haveH¯n−Hn⟂=op​(1)\bar{H}_{n}-H^{\perp}_{n}=o_{p}(1).
- (C8)

Oracle dyadic CLT.Define the centered oracle orthogonal scoreψ¯k,e⟂=ψk,e⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)−E​{ψk,e⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)∣𝒢n}\bar{\psi}^{\perp}_{k,e}=\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})-E\{\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\},
and letΩn⟂=Nn−1​∑k=1K∑e∈IkVar⁡{ψk,e⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)∣𝒢n}.\Omega^{\perp}_{n}=N_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\operatorname{Var}\{\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\}.

The eigenvalues ofΩn⟂\Omega^{\perp}_{n}are bounded away from zero and
infinity, and for everyϵ>0\epsilon>0,Nn−1​∑k=1K∑e∈IkE​[‖ψ¯k,e⟂‖2​𝟏​{‖ψ¯k,e⟂‖>ϵ​Nn}∣𝒢n]→0N_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\left[\|\bar{\psi}^{\perp}_{k,e}\|^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\|\bar{\psi}^{\perp}_{k,e}\|>\epsilon\sqrt{N_{n}}\}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\right]\to 0

in probability.
- (C9)

Variance estimation.LetH^n⟂=−Nn−1​∑k∑e∈Ik∂𝜷ψk,e⟂​(𝜷~,ξ^−k)\widehat{H}^{\perp}_{n}=-N_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\partial_{\bm{\beta}}\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{\xi}^{-k}),
and assumeH^n⟂−Hn⟂=op​(1)\widehat{H}^{\perp}_{n}-H^{\perp}_{n}=o_{p}(1)and that a foldwise covariance estimatorΩ^n⟂\widehat{\Omega}^{\perp}_{n}satisfiesΩ^n⟂−Ωn⟂=op​(1)\widehat{\Omega}^{\perp}_{n}-\Omega^{\perp}_{n}=o_{p}(1).
Under correct Bernoulli-BPS specification with per-dyad mean-zero
scores, the usual outer-product estimator may be used. Under
fixed-design misspecification, the naive outer product is not generally
consistent; thenΩ^n⟂\widehat{\Omega}^{\perp}_{n}must consistently estimate
the conditional Bernoulli varianceΩn⟂\Omega^{\perp}_{n}.

ThenNn​(𝜷~−𝜷∘)=(Hn⟂)−1​Nn−1/2​∑k=1K∑e∈Ikψ¯k,e⟂+op​(1).\sqrt{N_{n}}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ})=(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-1}N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\bar{\psi}^{\perp}_{k,e}+o_{p}(1).

Consequently, ifHn⟂→H⟂H^{\perp}_{n}\to H^{\perp}andΩn⟂→Ω⟂\Omega^{\perp}_{n}\to\Omega^{\perp},
then, unconditionally over the dyad draw, the random split, and the
agent refits,Nn​(𝜷~−𝜷∘)⇒N​(0,(H⟂)−1​Ω⟂​(H⟂)−⊤).\sqrt{N_{n}}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ})\Rightarrow N\left(0,(H^{\perp})^{-1}\Omega^{\perp}(H^{\perp})^{-\top}\right).

Equivalently, without deterministic matrix limits,[(Hn⟂)−1​Ωn⟂​(Hn⟂)−⊤]−1/2​Nn​(𝛃~−𝛃∘)⇒N​(0,Id)[(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-1}\Omega^{\perp}_{n}(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-\top}]^{-1/2}\sqrt{N_{n}}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ})\Rightarrow N(0,I_{d}).
Moreover, withV^n=(H^n⟂)−1​Ω^n⟂​(H^n⟂)−⊤\widehat{V}_{n}=(\widehat{H}^{\perp}_{n})^{-1}\widehat{\Omega}^{\perp}_{n}(\widehat{H}^{\perp}_{n})^{-\top},
for every fixed contrasta∈ℝda\in\mathbb{R}^{d}with0<lim infna⊤​(Hn⟂)−1​Ωn⟂​(Hn⟂)−⊤​a0<\liminf_{n}a^{\top}(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-1}\Omega^{\perp}_{n}(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-\top}a,Nn​a⊤​(𝜷~−𝜷∘)a⊤​V^n​a⇒N​(0,1),\frac{\sqrt{N_{n}}\,a^{\top}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ})}{\sqrt{a^{\top}\widehat{V}_{n}a}}\Rightarrow N(0,1),

so the Wald intervala⊤​𝛃~±z1−α/2​a⊤​V^n​a/Nna^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{a^{\top}\widehat{V}_{n}a/N_{n}}has coverage1−α+o​(1)1-\alpha+o(1). If(C1)–(C9)hold uniformly over a
class𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}, including the conditional CLT and variance
consistency, then the coverage is uniform over𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}.

## Proof.

By(C2)and the definition of𝜷∘\bm{\beta}^{\circ},Ψn⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)=Ψns​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)=0\Psi^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})=\Psi^{s}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})=0,
equivalently∑k∑e∈IkE​{ψk,e⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)∣𝒢n}=0\sum_{k}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\{\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\}=0.
This is an aggregate projection equation; it does not require
per-dyad mean-zero scores.
We first expand the estimating equation at the target. WithΔk,e=ψk,e⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ^−k)−ψk,e⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)\Delta_{k,e}=\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\widehat{\xi}^{-k})-\psi^{\perp}_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ}),Nn−1/2​Sn⟂​(𝜷∘)=Nn−1/2​∑k=1K∑e∈Ikψ¯k,e⟂+R1​n+R2​n,N_{n}^{-1/2}S^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ})=N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\bar{\psi}^{\perp}_{k,e}+R_{1n}+R_{2n},

whereR1​n=Nn−1/2​∑k∑e∈Ik[Δk,e−E​{Δk,e∣𝒯n,k}]R_{1n}=N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}[\Delta_{k,e}-E\{\Delta_{k,e}\mid\mathcal{T}_{n,k}\}]andR2​n=Nn−1/2​∑k∑e∈IkE​{Δk,e∣𝒯n,k}R_{2n}=N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\{\Delta_{k,e}\mid\mathcal{T}_{n,k}\}.
The oracle deterministic mean vanishes because of the aggregate moment
equation above.
By(C6),R1​n=op​(1)R_{1n}=o_{p}(1).
It remains to controlR2​nR_{2n}. Conditional on𝒯n,k\mathcal{T}_{n,k},
the nuisance estimateξ^−k\widehat{\xi}^{-k}is fixed and the validation
dyads inIkI_{k}are independent Bernoulli draws. Hence∑e∈IkE​{Δk,e∣𝒯n,k}=Nn,k​{Ψn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ^−k)−Ψn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)}.\sum_{e\in I_{k}}E\{\Delta_{k,e}\mid\mathcal{T}_{n,k}\}=N_{n,k}\left\{\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\widehat{\xi}^{-k})-\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})\right\}.

By the second-order expansion in(C3),Ψn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ^−k)−Ψn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)=∂ξΨn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)​[ξ^−k−ξ∘]+Op​(‖ξ^−k−ξ∘‖ℋn2).\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\widehat{\xi}^{-k})-\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})=\partial_{\xi}\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})[\widehat{\xi}^{-k}-\xi^{\circ}]+O_{p}(\|\widehat{\xi}^{-k}-\xi^{\circ}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}^{2}).

The first-order term is zero by foldwise Neyman orthogonality, so‖Ψn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ^−k)−Ψn,k⟂​(𝜷∘,ξ∘)‖=Op​(rn2)\|\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\widehat{\xi}^{-k})-\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\xi^{\circ})\|=O_{p}(r_{n}^{2})uniformly inkk. Since∑kNn,k=Nn\sum_{k}N_{n,k}=N_{n},‖R2​n‖≤Nn−1/2​∑k=1KNn,k​Op​(rn2)=Op​(Nn​rn2)=op​(1)\|R_{2n}\|\leq N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}N_{n,k}O_{p}(r_{n}^{2})=O_{p}(\sqrt{N_{n}}r_{n}^{2})=o_{p}(1)

by(C5). Consequently,Nn−1/2​Sn⟂​(𝜷∘)=Nn−1/2​∑k∑e∈Ikψ¯k,e⟂+op​(1)N_{n}^{-1/2}S^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ})=N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\bar{\psi}^{\perp}_{k,e}+o_{p}(1).
By(C8), conditional on𝒢n\mathcal{G}_{n},Nn−1/2​∑k∑e∈Ikψ¯k,e⟂⇒N​(0,Ωn⟂)N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\bar{\psi}^{\perp}_{k,e}\Rightarrow N(0,\Omega^{\perp}_{n})in the triangular-array sense; ifΩn⟂→Ω⟂\Omega^{\perp}_{n}\to\Omega^{\perp}this isN​(0,Ω⟂)N(0,\Omega^{\perp}), and the same holds unconditionally because the
conditional characteriztic functions converge in probability and are
uniformly bounded.
Next, sinceSn⟂​(𝜷~)=0S^{\perp}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}})=0, the integral form of
Taylor’s theorem gives0=Sn⟂​(𝜷∘)+[∫01∂𝜷Sn⟂​(𝜷∘+s​(𝜷~−𝜷∘))​d​s]​(𝜷~−𝜷∘)=Sn⟂​(𝜷∘)−Nn​H¯n​(𝜷~−𝜷∘),0=S^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ})+\left[\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\bm{\beta}}S^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ}+s(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ}))ds\right](\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ})=S^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ})-N_{n}\bar{H}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ}),

soNn​(𝜷~−𝜷∘)=H¯n−1​Nn−1/2​Sn⟂​(𝜷∘)\sqrt{N_{n}}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ})=\bar{H}_{n}^{-1}N_{n}^{-1/2}S^{\perp}_{n}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ}).
By(C7),H¯n=Hn⟂+op​(1)\bar{H}_{n}=H^{\perp}_{n}+o_{p}(1)withHn⟂H^{\perp}_{n}uniformly nonsingular, soH¯n−1=(Hn⟂)−1+op​(1)\bar{H}_{n}^{-1}=(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-1}+o_{p}(1), andNn​(𝜷~−𝜷∘)=(Hn⟂)−1​Nn−1/2​∑k=1K∑e∈Ikψ¯k,e⟂+op​(1).\sqrt{N_{n}}(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{\circ})=(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-1}N_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{e\in I_{k}}\bar{\psi}^{\perp}_{k,e}+o_{p}(1).

IfHn⟂→H⟂H^{\perp}_{n}\to H^{\perp}andΩn⟂→Ω⟂\Omega^{\perp}_{n}\to\Omega^{\perp}, Slutsky’s
theorem yields the stated normal limit; otherwise the asymptotic linear
representation and(C8)give the self-normalized form.
Finally, by(C9),V^n=(Hn⟂)−1​Ωn⟂​(Hn⟂)−⊤+op​(1)\widehat{V}_{n}=(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-1}\Omega^{\perp}_{n}(H^{\perp}_{n})^{-\top}+o_{p}(1), so for every fixed contrastaawith
nondegenerate limiting variance Slutsky’s theorem gives the scalar normal
limit, and the Wald interval has pointwise coverage1−α+o​(1)1-\alpha+o(1). If
the assumptions hold uniformly over𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}, the same argument with
uniform versions of the CLT, the nuisance-rate bounds, nonsingularity, and
variance consistency gives uniform coverage.
∎

## Remark S2(Node-clustered variance under shared-node dependence).

Condition (C9) takes the meatΩ^n⟂\widehat{\Omega}^{\perp}_{n}of the sandwich to be a consistent
estimator of the score covarianceΩn⟂\Omega^{\perp}_{n}. When the dyads are conditionally independent the
per-dyad outer productNn−1​∑es^e​s^e⊤N_{n}^{-1}\sum_{e}\widehat{s}_{e}\widehat{s}_{e}^{\top}, withs^e=(Ye−p^e)​ze\widehat{s}_{e}=(Y_{e}-\widehat{p}_{e})z_{e}the estimated score contribution of dyadee, is consistent. When
two dyads that share a node are dependent, as for the correlation-thresholded networks of the main text
where edges sharing an asset are dependent, the score covariances between node-sharing dyads do not vanish
and the outer product understatesΩn⟂\Omega^{\perp}_{n}. The consistent replacement is the node-clustered
(dyadic-robust) estimatorΩ^ndy=Nn−1​∑e∑e′𝟏​{e∩e′≠∅}​s^e​s^e′⊤,\widehat{\Omega}^{\,\mathrm{dy}}_{n}=N_{n}^{-1}\sum_{e}\sum_{e^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}\{e\cap e^{\prime}\neq\varnothing\}\,\widehat{s}_{e}\,\widehat{s}_{e^{\prime}}^{\top},

summing the outer products of all dyad pairs that share at least one node. Under the dyadic-dependence model
in which dyads with no common node are conditionally independent,Ω^ndy−Ωn⟂=op​(1)\widehat{\Omega}^{\,\mathrm{dy}}_{n}-\Omega^{\perp}_{n}=o_{p}(1)by the cluster-robust argument with the nodes as
overlapping clusters, so (C9) holds withΩ^n⟂=Ω^ndy\widehat{\Omega}^{\perp}_{n}=\widehat{\Omega}^{\,\mathrm{dy}}_{n}and the Wald interval retains its coverage. This is the variance reported for the S&P 500 network in the main
text; it reduces to the per-dyad outer product when node-sharing dyads are uncorrelated.

## Remark S3(Verification of the orthogonal construction).

The single load-bearing step in Theorem 1c is that the explicit correction of(C4),ck,e=Ψ˙γ,n,ks​Γ˙γ,n,k−1​gk,ec_{k,e}=\dot{\Psi}^{s}_{\gamma,n,k}\,\dot{\Gamma}_{\gamma,n,k}^{-1}\,g_{k,e}, satisfies the
target-preservation condition(C2)and the foldwise Neyman-orthogonality
condition(C3); the remainder of the proof is the standard asymptotic-linearity
argument. Both follow in one line from the zero-conditional-mean nuisance scoreΓn,k​(γ∘)=0\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma^{\circ})=0. For(C2), the correction inherits that zero
mean,E​{ck,e​(𝜷∘,γ∘)∣𝒢n}=Ψ˙γ,n,ks​Γ˙γ,n,k−1​Γn,k​(γ∘)=0,E\{c_{k,e}(\bm{\beta}^{\circ},\gamma^{\circ})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\}=\dot{\Psi}^{s}_{\gamma,n,k}\,\dot{\Gamma}_{\gamma,n,k}^{-1}\,\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma^{\circ})=0,

so the orthogonal score keeps the BPS target𝜷∘\bm{\beta}^{\circ}. For(C3),
differentiatingΨn,k⟂=Ψn,ks−E​{ck,e∣𝒢n}\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}=\Psi^{s}_{n,k}-E\{c_{k,e}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\}inγ\gammaatγ∘\gamma^{\circ}and using∂γ[Γ˙γ,n,k−1​Γn,k]γ∘=Γ˙γ,n,k−1​Γ˙γ,n,k=I\partial_{\gamma}[\dot{\Gamma}_{\gamma,n,k}^{-1}\Gamma_{n,k}]_{\gamma^{\circ}}=\dot{\Gamma}_{\gamma,n,k}^{-1}\dot{\Gamma}_{\gamma,n,k}=Igives∂γΨn,k⟂=Ψ˙γ,n,ks−Ψ˙γ,n,ks=0\partial_{\gamma}\Psi^{\perp}_{n,k}=\dot{\Psi}^{s}_{\gamma,n,k}-\dot{\Psi}^{s}_{\gamma,n,k}=0, the
partialling-out identity of two-step semiparametric estimation. The construction can therefore be

## Remark S4(Which agents satisfy the nuisance-rate condition (C5)).

Condition (C5) asks forNn​rn2→0\sqrt{N_{n}}\,r_{n}^{2}\to 0in the score-scale norm∥⋅∥ℋn\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}. Since a logit-feature deviation equals the relative probability
deviation on the sparse scalepe≍ρnp_{e}\asymp\rho_{n},rn2r_{n}^{2}is the probability-scale dyadic
mean-squared error of the agent divided byρn2\rho_{n}^{2}. An agent with a fixed number of parameters,
each informed byΘ​(n2)\Theta(n^{2})dyads, has probability-scale errorO​(ρn/n2)O(\rho_{n}/n^{2})and hencern2=O​(1/(n2​ρn))r_{n}^{2}=O(1/(n^{2}\rho_{n})), soNn​rn2=O​(K2/(n​ρn))→0\sqrt{N_{n}}\,r_{n}^{2}=O(K^{2}/(n\sqrt{\rho_{n}}))\to 0; theKK-block
model with fixedKKis of this type. An agent withΘ​(n)\Theta(n)node-level parameters, each
informed by onlyΘ​(n)\Theta(n)dyads, has relative error≍(n​ρn)−1/2\asymp(n\rho_{n})^{-1/2}per dyad and sorn2≍1/(n​ρn)r_{n}^{2}\asymp 1/(n\rho_{n})up to dimension and polylog factors, givingNn​rn2≍1/ρn\sqrt{N_{n}}\,r_{n}^{2}\asymp 1/\sqrt{\rho_{n}}, which does not vanish in the sparse regime; the
adjacency-spectral (GRDPG) and expected-degree (Chung–Lu) agents are of this type. Theorem 1c
therefore delivers the unconditional limit for finite-dimensional agents. ForΘ​(n)\Theta(n)-parameter
agents the second-order condition is not available in the sparse regime, and the relevant guarantee is
the conditional limit of SectionS1, which conditions on the fitted features and requires
no agent-rate condition.

## S7Local minimax lower bound for mechanism tracking (Theorem 3b)

This section gives the full statement and proof of the local minimax lower bound matching the tracking upper bound of SectionS3, together with its separated-switch
and switch-regret corollaries. The argument is a sparse Bernoulli-logit reduction: the
per-dyad divergence bound of Lemma 1 (SectionS2) controls the information in one snapshot, and
the lower bounds follow by Fano, Bretagnolle–Huber, and a stopped-likelihood argument across
the conditionally independent post-switch snapshots.

## Corollary 3b.1(Separated-switch reliable-delay lower bound).

Assume the conditions of Theorem3b. Suppose the
dynamic class containsSSseparated hard switch episodes: after each switchsℓs_{\ell}, conditional on the past up tosℓs_{\ell}, the post-switch law contains
the sameJJ-point local BPS packing as in
Theorem3b, the corresponding post-switch testing
windows are disjoint, and each post-switch regime lasts at least1+⌈C​LJ,α/(N​κ2)⌉1+\lceil CL_{J,\alpha}/(N\kappa^{2})\rceilsnapshots. If the procedure is required to make anα\alpha-reliable local
declaration after each switch,supPP​{r^τℓ≠rsℓ+∣ℱsℓ}≤α\sup_{P}P\{\widehat{r}_{\tau_{\ell}}\neq r_{s_{\ell}+}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s_{\ell}}\}\leq\alphaalmost surely for eachℓ\ell, theninfr^supP∈𝒫n,T​(S,κ,N)EP​∑ℓ=1S(τℓ−sℓ)≥c​S​LJ,αN​κ2.\inf_{\widehat{r}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n,T}(S,\kappa,N)}E_{P}\sum_{\ell=1}^{S}(\tau_{\ell}-s_{\ell})\geq c\frac{SL_{J,\alpha}}{N\kappa^{2}}.

## Proof.

Condition on the historyℱsℓ\mathcal{F}_{s_{\ell}}immediately before switchsℓs_{\ell}. By assumption, the conditional post-switch experiment contains the
same least-favorableJJ-point BPS packing as in
Theorem3b. Applying the reliable declaration-delay
part of that theorem conditionally onℱsℓ\mathcal{F}_{s_{\ell}}givesEP​(τℓ−sℓ∣ℱsℓ)≥c​LJ,α/(N​κ2)E_{P}(\tau_{\ell}-s_{\ell}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s_{\ell}})\geq cL_{J,\alpha}/(N\kappa^{2}).
Taking expectations and summing over theSSdisjoint episodes gives the claim,
and the supremum overPPwith the infimum over admissible procedures completes
the proof.
∎

## Corollary 3b.2(Switch-regret lower bound for BPS coefficient predictors).

Assume the conditions of Theorem3band the separated
hard-episode condition of Corollary3b.1. Restrict
attention to predictable BPS coefficient predictors𝛃^t|t−1∈Θ0\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{t|t-1}\in\Theta_{0}measurable with respect toA1:t−1A_{1:t-1}. ForNt=Nt​(𝛃t∘)N_{t}=N_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ}), define
the information-normalized BPS riskR¯t​(𝜷)=1Nt​∑e∈EtKL⁡{Bern⁡(q𝜷t∘,t,e),Bern⁡(q𝜷,t,e)}.\bar{R}_{t}(\bm{\beta})=\frac{1}{N_{t}}\sum_{e\in E_{t}}\operatorname{KL}\!\left\{\operatorname{Bern}(q_{\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ},t,e}),\operatorname{Bern}(q_{\bm{\beta},t,e})\right\}.

Theninf𝜷^supP∈𝒫n,T​(S,κ,N)EP​∑t=1TNt​{R¯t​(𝜷^t|t−1)−R¯t​(𝜷t∘)}≥c​S​LJ.\inf_{\widehat{\bm{\beta}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n,T}(S,\kappa,N)}E_{P}\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}\{\bar{R}_{t}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{t|t-1})-\bar{R}_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})\}\geq cSL_{J}.

SinceR¯t​(𝛃t∘)=0\bar{R}_{t}(\bm{\beta}_{t}^{\circ})=0in the least-favorable family, the displayed
quantity is the cumulative excess one-step BPS log-score, an ordinary expected
predictive-regret lower bound. The factorlog⁡(1/α)\log(1/\alpha)belongs to reliable
hard localization and is not part of unconstrained expected predictive regret;
the stationary parametric term of order(J+1)​log⁡(T​N)(J+1)\log(TN)requires a separate
stationary BPS regret lower-bound lemma.
